#7263. Staff Application Security Engineer at Fingerprint

Fingerprint empowers enterprises to detect and stop online fraud with the world’s most accurate device intelligence.  We lead our industry with bleeding-edge identification capabilities and work on turning new ideas and discoveries in the fraud detection space into reality. Our customers range from innovative startups to leading enterprise companies, including Plaid, Dropbox, and Booking.com.

Fingerprint is a globally dispersed, 100% remote company. We were named on on the 2026 Forbes Best Startup Employers list and ranked #803 on the 2026 Inc. 5000 list of America’s fastest-growing private companies.

We have raised $77M and are backed by Craft Ventures (Tesla, Facebook, Airbnb ), Nexus Venture Partners ( Postman, Apollo.io, MinIO, Druva) and Uncorrelated Ventures ( Redis, Rollbar,  Gradle).

Fingerprint's device intelligence platform processes billions of identification events for some of the world's most security-conscious companies — financial institutions, fraud prevention operators, and enterprises that rely on us to stop attackers. That makes our own application security a product feature, not a back-office function: our customers' adversaries are, by definition, motivated to probe what we build.

As we scale with large enterprise customers, we're investing in application security as a dedicated, first-class engineering discipline. Security is already part of how our teams build — scanning in the pipeline, a Cloud Platform team that treats security as a partner, and a mature compliance posture — and we're now standing up a focused AppSec function, led by a Senior Manager, Application Security who owns the program and strategy, to take it to the next level. You will be the founding senior engineer of that function — the person who turns strategy into a working secure SDLC, finds and fixes what matters, and raises the security bar across engineering through technical depth, tooling, and influence.

This is a hands-on Staff role. You will design, build, and operate — not just advise.

Threat Modeling, Security Reviews & Vulnerability Management

Incident Response & Detection (application layer)

This is the senior individual-contributor counterpart to the Senior Manager, Application Security. The manager owns the program, the strategy, and the team; you own the technical depth and the execution. The two roles are designed to work as a pair: you'll bring the findings, the engineering judgment, and the ground truth from inside the codebase, and together you'll decide what to prioritize.

As a Staff engineer, you are expected to operate with a high degree of independence — scoping ambiguous problems, setting technical direction for AppSec, and driving multi-quarter initiatives without needing day-to-day direction. As the founding engineer of a dedicated AppSec function, the patterns, tooling, and standards you establish will be the foundation the practice grows on.

This is not a role for someone who wants to primarily write policy or manage a program from a distance. It's for an engineer who finds real bugs, builds real tooling, and earns the trust of other engineers by making their code better.

At Fingerprint, we believe in pay transparency — it's part of how we operate as a distributed, remote-first team.  Salaries are based on function, level, and geographic location, benchmarked against similar stage growth companies.

Because this role is open across the United States and can be based anywhere our team members work from, the salary range reflects that geographic breadth: $177k - $240k

#7261. TikTok updates its AI video ad tools

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company’s Symphony platform now has access to the latest model from Dreamina Seedance, offering brands expanded creation options.

TikTok has added some new elements to its artificial intelligence-powered Symphony ad creation tools, including advanced video generation using the latest models, an updated Symphony API and broader access to AI usage tokens.

First, TikTok added more capacity to generate video ads using the latest Dreamina Seedance AI models within its Symphony Agent tool.

As shown in this example sequence, TikTok’s Symphony now has access to the updated visual generation capacity of Dreamina Seedance to generate better-looking ads based on expanded prompts, with users able to integrate example videos and ads.

TikTok said advertisers will now be able to upload up to 50 multimodal references (images, videos, and audio), up from 9 previously, while the updated Seedance models will now be able to generate up to 30 seconds of video for each prompt, up from a previous 15-second maximum.

TikTok said the latest Dreamina Seedance model is also able to generate sharper, more realistic imagery and offer better lighting and character consistency across video frames.

The viability of AI-generated video on TikTok will vary, but it could be worth experimenting with these new video generation tools, based on expanded creative inputs, to see what the system can generate.

TikTok also launched an updated Symphony API, which will enable advertisers to generate content using Symphony’s tools within their own creative management tools.

The company also expanded access to its BytePlus tokens, which will enable more use of its Symphony tools for visual generation.

“Starting November 9, advertisers outside North America can purchase BytePlus tokens to access Dreamina Seedance AI models through Symphony, enabling faster video generation, higher-quality output, and more formats,” TikTok said.

BytePlus is the enterprise technology arm of TikTok parent company ByteDance, and it manages all of the company’s AI tools and systems.

These updates could provide additional considerations for advertisers heading into peak shopping season.

We are currently hiring a Staff Security Engineer within the Imunify360 product.

As an individual contributor, you won't have direct reports or people management responsibilities. All security research and testing are conducted within authorized boundaries, with clearly defined scope and responsible disclosure practices.

Your mission is to strengthen Imunify360's ability to detect, prevent, and respond to emerging threats, delivering measurable security improvements that protect customers at scale.

Check out our website for more information: https://imunify360.com/

As a Staff Engineer, you'll be a hands-on technical leader working across the entire product, focusing on the most critical and high-impact challenges as priorities evolve. You'll drive technical decisions, improve architecture, establish engineering standards, and help teams build sustainable solutions.

This is an individual contributor role, without direct people management responsibilities. You'll remain actively involved in development, spending approximately 30–50% of your time writing production code, rather than focusing solely on architectural design.

#7255. Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text, with Sponsored Product ads in the replies

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text message and the thread where those orders get placed will carry ads. Sponsored Products are set to appear inside those conversations, free to advertisers for the time being. Purchases the agent completes on its own will stay ad-free.

The texting option went live today for a small, invite-only group of U.S. customers. Amazon plans to open it to millions more over the coming weeks and anyone keen to jump the queue can sign up for the company’s text updates and wait for an invite.

Once in, customers link a phone number, accept the terms and get a new contact called “Alexa Shopping.” From there they message it the way they’d message a friend. Reorder the laundry detergent from last month. Build a grocery shop from a photo of a recipe. Find a birthday present that arrives by Saturday. Alexa for Shopping searches, compares options and texts back with suggestions, then adds the chosen items to the cart and checks out when told to. Customers who’d rather not be asked can switch on auto-buy and let the agent pull the trigger when a price hits their target.

The ad opportunity sits in the discovery stage of that text exchange. When the agent texts back a set of options, a Sponsored Product can be one of them, which puts brands inside one of the most personal channels on a phone with room for a handful of products at most. Amazon has kept ads to that moment, while the customer is still choosing. Slipping one into a purchase the agent makes is a bigger gamble. After all, no one who asks Alexa to reorder dog food when the price drops wants to find out the bag on the doorstep was the one a brand paid for rather than the one they’d have picked.

Amazon isn’t charging for these placements yet, which gives it a cheap way to test how much advertising people will put up with in a text thread — and there’s no shortage of people to test it on. More than 400 million customers have used Alexa for Shopping in the past 12 months, according to Amazon, and use of its agentic features has grown more than eightfold since January.

“Any new ad placement is generally seen as an interesting test and this approach from Amazon is no different,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Sousa-Rauto. “Amazon are already well known for putting ad slots on the order confirmation page, which whilst generates ad revenue, could be considered non-incremental for most advertisers. As agentic commerce meets advertising, the definition of an impression is critical as well as the reporting and measurement, to understand if it is truly driving efficiency for brands.”

The feature is designed to work across Amazon’s shopping app, website and Alexa-enabled devices, while those text conversations can carry between them. A request started over text carries into the Amazon Shopping app, the site and Alexa-enabled devices and back again. People can send photos of something spotted while scrolling and ask for lookalikes. They can set price alerts or tell the agent to watch for new launches, with Amazon’s own example being a budget when Nike drops new running shoes. The agent also keeps a running tally of preferences. Mention veganism or marathon training once and it shapes the recommendations that follow. Through Shop Direct, it can also surface and buy products from stores beyond Amazon.

“Customers often jot down what they need the moment they think of it in their notes, in their messages, in their group chats,” said Rajiv Mehta, vp of conversational shopping at Amazon. “Now with Alexa for Shopping, your personal shopping agent is just a text away. Message it what you need and Alexa goes to work on your behalf—searching, comparing, and buying so you can get on with your day.”

This text thread is the latest installment of Amazon’s evolving ad business.

In March, Sponsored Products and Brands prompts arrived, followed by Alexa for Shopping, which replaced Rufus in May and took over the main search bar in the app. This was then followed by Alexa+ Agentic Ads which was announced at Cannes Lions this summer, before the company’s advertiser platform was rebranded as Amazon Ads Agent. During the same month, Amazon blocked Meta’s AI shopping agent Muse, stating that the agent accessed its platform without prior notification, failure to properly identify itself and reportedly captured and stored customers’ details.

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

#7250. ChatGPT is about to show brands their mention count

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Marketers could soon find out how often ChatGPT users talk about them.

OpenAI is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers and how many clicks those appearances send to its site. The tool sits inside a wider Business Hub that hasn’t launched yet. Juozas Kaziukėnas, founder of e-commerce research firm Marketplace Pulse, posted a screenshot on LinkedIn last week (November 6) after spotting a banner for the unreleased feature.

The banner asks businesses to “Claim your business in ChatGPT”, according to two separate sources with knowledge of its workings. Once claimed, a business can track its mentions and keep its products and business information current. Claiming also unlocks what OpenAI calls a business agent, which answers customer questions using contact the business supplies. Getting in requires proof of authority to represent the business. Kaziukėnas said in the post that connecting a Shopify store is one way to do that.

According to those sources, the dashboard logs impressions and clicks each day and plots them on a chart. It stops well short of what search marketers get from Google. Prompts stay hidden. So does user data and there’s no ranking against competitors. Kaziukėnas said he first saw traces of the feature in August.

Whatever OpenAI ships will land in a crowded market. Generative engine optimization vendors such as Profound and Adthena sell brands a read on their ChatGPT visibility, mostly by running prompts through the chatbot at scale and tracking what comes back. None of them has OpenAI’s own counts, though the beta dashboard also omits the prompts and competitor rankings those vendors sell. Kaziukėnas expects the two to work in tandem, with outside tools tracking prompts and OpenAI’s first-party data showing whether GEO strategies are working.

On paper the dashboard sits apart from ChatGPT ads as it deals in organic answers. Even so, OpenAI’s ads business has arguably the most to gain from its arrival. Once ChatGPT visibility shows up as a daily number, marketers will want that number to climb and the lever the company charges for it is a sponsored placement sitting next to the answer. Google ran the same play years ago. Business Profile listings put local businesses on its map. Search Console showed site owners how they performed in organic search and both gave Google’s sales teams an easier route to selling those businesses ads. OpenAI already has a self-serve ads manager and a measurement pixel. Business Hub fills in the organic side.

Ads in ChatGPT went into testing in the U.S. in February. By September OpenAI was claiming a $1 billion annualized run rate, reached in under 200 days, and in late October it said more than 100,000 advertisers had run campaigns across 89 countries. Sustaining that growth means signing up businesses that have never bought ChatGPT ads and a claimed listing gives OpenAI a line to them.

OpenAI declined to comment for this story.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

Apple is placing strict limits on its staffers’ AI token consumption. Should the marketing sector take a similar path?

Q4 credits may bring advertisers to Reddit, but concerns about measurement and its ad capabilities make it harder to secure year-round spend.

#7248. Media Buying Briefing: Forrester’s Wave report finds growing homogeneity among the 12 biggest agencies

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Buying Briefing covers the latest in agency news and media buying for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Monday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

The latest iteration of Forrester’s Wave Report on media agencies — rather, media management services, as the research firm puts it — is out, assessing the 12 biggest agency groups currently operating today that are of interest to Forrester’s clients. And guess what? Consolidation has made the biggest of them look more alike than ever.

“I was anticipating more differentiation in the marketplace as a result of of the consolidations and acquisitions that took place,” said Pattisall, referring to things like Omnicom’s acquisition of IPG and Publicis’ various tech and segment purchases, as well as PE-backed acquisitions like Wpromote’s purchase of Giant Spoon. “I was anticipating that we would see more separation, if you will. Once you look outside of scale and size, then the differences are much more subtle.”

To be clear, there are winners and losers as the report lays out in an opening Venn diagram. Three firms were listed as “leaders,” according to a scoring system based on 19 criteria ranging from “agentic media buying” to “customer lifecycle management” and “global delivery strategy”. They were, of course, the Big Three (Omnicom, Publicis and WPP). Dentsu and Havas led the “strong performers” group, along with Wpromote, PMG, Tinuiti and Horizon Media. Filling out the “contenders” category were Stagwell, Dept and Rise (a Quad agency).

Private-equity-backed shops like Wpromote fared well, and Forrester’s description gave it props for pursuing agentic media buying as well as AEO abilities that “unites paid, owned, and earned authority-building with the measurement of visibility, sentiment, and traffic quality.”

“We valued their measurement capabilities,” Pattisall said. “We valued their precision capabilities. We valued their planning acumen, their optimization mindset — all of that plays very well for that group. It is the reason that you see relatively smaller organizations in the ‘strong performer’ grouping.”

Reacting to its position as the highest-ranking independent in terms of strength of offering in the report, Wpromote CEO Andrea Bendzick cited the big opportunity for independents.

“We’re at a point where the industry is being reshaped by AI, new ways of reaching consumers and undeniable pressure to connect marketing investments to business outcomes,” said Bendzick. “Independent agencies are well positioned to invest in those emerging areas and build their businesses around where clients are going, not where they’ve been.”

Other reaction to the report was mixed, with Publicis demurring from any comment (it was the only agency group that declined to participate in the report’s outreach for insights and explanations, as well as client-side input). With its run of successful client wins and poaches, the argument can be made that it doesn’t need to go out there and prove itself to prospective clients through a Forrester report, but it also is perceived as arrogant by some in the industry.

Regardless, Forrester’s scoring credits the holdco’s media buying, principal media, and data and marketing sciences capabilities — and awarded Publicis the highest average score in strategy, a 4.4 out of 5. “These strengths plus its impressive media buying scale produce exceptional client media value. Customer lifecycle management also excels, with global client leads coordinating resource access and keeping implementation teams involved after launch,” reads the report. However, of the Big Three, it also scored the only 1s (the lowest score) in “agentic media buying” due to a lack of “tangible pilots, scaled partnerships, or evidence of meaningful client impact, and SEO/AEO which “lacks proactive experimentation.”

Omnicom declared validation for its acquisition of IPG — and it received the highest score in “current offering,” at a 3.8 out of 5 — which Forrester credited it for being the largest buyer both domestically and globally. The holdco wasted no time in celebrating its solid ranking as a “leader”.

“In a marketplace being reshaped by AI, commerce and increasingly complex consumer journeys, we believe Forrester offers marketers a valuable perspective on what truly differentiates a leader: the ability to connect intelligence, technology, partnerships and buying power to deliver measurable business value,” said Omnicom Media CEO Florian Adamski in a prepared statement.

Regarding WPP, Pattisall noted that “their capabilities in the media marketplace exceed their perception in the marketplace,” due to the fact that Wall Street is still punishing the British-owned holdco for the sins of prior management. “What their stock price is isn’t going to drive their value to a client, and we’re measuring the latter and not the former,” he added.

“What’s increasingly clear is that competitive advantage in marketing will come not from who owns or accumulates the most data but from who can unlock the most intelligence from it and put that intelligence to work,” said Lauren Wetzel, global president, data and technology solutions at WPP.

Interestingly, Stagwell, which many do not consider a holding company — scored somewhat poorly in the Wave report, averaging a 2.4 in strategy and a 2.44 in current offering. Only Rise (scoring a 1.6 in strategy) and Dept (a 2.2 in strategy) scoring lower averages.

Pattisall in part attributed Stagwell’s lower score to “the newness of the Stagwell Media strategy,” which he credited with being “a very calculated and appropriate move to put together compatible capabilities to go to market at the group level, if if and when necessary.”

But it got dinged, he added, because “they are a relatively new organization. Their mission and their vision and their position in the marketplace is not as sharp as the others because they have not been operating and functioning at this level but for only a couple of months.”

Missing from the list because they either didn’t meet the $3.1 billion billings threshold or because Forrester clients weren’t as interested in them were S4 Capital’s Monks and other independents including Crossmedia or Brainlabs.

With midterm elections finally over — and one can once again watch Jeopardy without the constant bombardment of negative political ads — estimates for total  political ad spending vary from a low of $11.4 billion by AdImpact, which is seen as the most reliable figure, to OpenSecret’s $11.9 billion, which it said it based on an analysis of Federal Election Commission disclosure data. Either way it’s a record — and it seems to indicate that the 2028 presidential election cycle will raise the bar even higher. Whether candidates find other ways to spend their money besides 30-second TV spots remains to be seen.

“The question CMOs are asking isn’t whether LiveRamp still works. It’s whether they want their customer data running through infrastructure owned by an agency holding company that competes with their other agencies. A neutrality pledge is a promise; neutrality by design is an architecture. More brands want their identity and audience data to stay in their own warehouse, under their own control, so they can work with any agency or partner without routing everything through one company’s pipes. The deal didn’t create that shift, but it has definitely sped it up.”

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

#7243. WTF is the AI training opt-out policy token?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Some of the largest AI tech companies — including Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI — have agreed to support a new mechanism that lets publishers opt out of having their content used to train AI models, in just a few lines of text.

The proposed standard, called a “policy token” for AI training, comes as publishers seek more control over how tech companies scrape and use their content. They’ve tried blocking AI bots, pursuing legal action and negotiating licensing deals.

At first glance, this AI training policy token appears to offer a straightforward fix. In reality, it highlights why the problem is so complicated.

The idea is that publishers can add a line to their robots.txt file — which websites use to tell web crawlers what content they are allowed to access — to express whether they want their content used for AI training.

Here’s what the policy token looks like, as written in robots.txt: User-Agent: __usage_ai-training

Below that, publishers can write “disallow” or “allow,” to show which crawlers can scrape and which can’t. In the “allow” line, publishers can also offer certain exceptions, such as letting crawlers scrape a press kit.

Typically, publishers need to list each crawler individually in robots.txt and give permission (or not) to crawl their sites. The new AI training policy token would allow publishers to do so in one step. Instead of managing separate instructions for every crawler, they can set broad preferences to participating AI and tech companies.

The proposal was written by Kevin Kelley, a technical lead for standards at Anthropic. Kelley submitted the proposal to the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF), an internet standards development organization, on Oct. 30.

Technically, the standard is not official yet. The IETF launched an effort known as AI Preferences (AIPREF) to develop new standards for website owners to express preferences about how their content gets used for AI. Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI have all been involved in the group.

While the largest search and AI providers have agreed to support this policy token, it still needs to be deliberated on by the IETF. And the fact that these major companies have jumped to support this draft raises some concerns, said Alex Springer, technical lead at SPUR, a coalition of publishers working to develop AI standards.

“The IETF exists to allow for individuals to work in public to form consensus about the standards and protocols that make the internet work. The way that the platforms have approached this by announcing their unilateral support of a ‘standard’ that is an unreviewed draft is the antithesis of that,” he said. “They appear to have decided to unilaterally wield their collective weight in a concerted effort to force a new de facto standard in place, one that falls far short of what those content owners require.”

Anthropic says it is rolling out adoption of the signal now.

In a blog post, OpenAI said it would honor the policy token “in the coming months.” It noted that the proposed mechanism would not replace its existing crawler controls.

Google wrote in its own blog post that it aims to support this policy token in 2027. It noted that it builds on the options it already offers publishers, such as Google-Extended, a web crawler control implemented in 2023 that allows publishers to use robots.txt to restrict their content from being used to train Google’s Gemini AI models and ground responses in certain AI products.

Microsoft wrote in a blog post that not only would it comply with the AI training policy token but that it would also offer publishers the ability to opt out of AI grounding, or when crawlers scrape sites to find real-time information. These preferences will replace the existing Microsoft preferences “NoArchive” and “NoCache,” for AI training and grounding respectively, starting Feb. 1, 2027.

Meta didn’t go as far as to say it would honor the policy token, but Andy Stone, a Meta company spokesperson, wrote on X: “Meta, too, strongly support publishers’ ability to express preferences about how their content is used for foundational generative AI training. We believe creating a common signal that conveys such preferences to multiple providers at once is an important step. And we look forward to working with other AI companies on the standards and their implementation across the industry.”

The proposal may appeal to publishers seeking standardized controls. But, like robots.txt, it offers no way to ensure AI and tech companies comply with publishers’ preferences.

Robots.txt preferences are infamously more of a suggestion than an enforceable doctrine.

In fact, data suggests that web crawlers rarely even check publishers’ robots.txt files when scraping their sites. Crawlers operated by the four companies backing the shared opt-out token made roughly 1 billion requests over the past 30 days — but just 0.15% of those requests were for robots.txt files, according to data shared with Digiday by AI visibility measurement firm ScalePost, which tracked activity across more than 50 publisher properties.

Paul Bannister, chief strategy officer at Raptive, called the AI training policy token “window dressing.”

“I think this is useless and is marketing, and far, far, far too late… It does nothing and doesn’t solve the problem at all,” he said.

AI tech companies have a lot of reasons to want to appear to appease publishers. They’re fighting a number of copyright infringement lawsuits, pressure from government regulators, and proposed legislation targeting crawlers.

Four major AI tech companies have said they will honor this AI training policy token, making this one of the first proposed standards with that kind of support.

While it remains to be seen if this will be the case in practice, the policy token could be another layer publishers have at their disposal to protect their content from web crawlers, in addition to mechanisms like blocking crawlers at the CDN level or using tools like TollBit.

“Even though it doesn’t keep all the thieves out, you’d still put a lock on your front door because it keeps some of the thieves out… You do lots and lots of things to minimize the chances that your house gets broken into, but you cannot prevent it completely,” Bannister said. “Is it one other arrow to put in your quiver of about 1,000 things you need to do? Sure.”

Many publishers have been working hard to improve their AI visibility, and some are selling GEO products to brands to boost their visibility in LLMs. The last thing those publishers want is to jeopardize their mentions and citations in those tools.

Of the four companies backing the policy token, Microsoft was the only one to explicitly address its implications for AI visibility. If a publisher does disallow crawlers for AI grounding, “you will see reduced brand visibility in AI experiences,” reads Microsoft’s blog post.

Google’s official documentation states that using Google-Extended to opt out of certain AI training and grounding uses does not affect a publisher’s visibility in Google Search. But publishers argue that other opt-out mechanisms, such as Google’s Search generative AI control, present a tougher choice. Without the AI-specific click data they’re waiting on, publishers can’t yet accurately assess how much traffic and visibility they risk losing by opting out.

The AI training policy token doesn’t have anything to do with AI inference (or AI usage), which is when an AI model generates responses or performs tasks by retrieving or processing content from publishers’ websites in real time. This has become core to most of the recent AI licensing deals between publishers and tech companies.

And even if an AI crawler abides by publishers’ preferences expressed in robots.txt, it doesn’t mean the AI provider won’t be able to get that data elsewhere, such as from other crawlers, Bannister said.

“None of them have said, ‘We won’t buy data from third parties that scrape the content that don’t obey this,’ or ‘We won’t take open source crawls like Common Crawl,’ Bannister said. “They’re going to get all the data anyway, just through a different mechanism, and so it’s completely useless.”

The draft also appears to reinforce the idea that AI companies can use publishers’ content for training by default, unless publishers explicitly tell them not to, Springer said. This “opt in unless opted out” concept risks legitimizing a system where crawlers can presume they have permission for AI training, rather than needing explicit consent, he argued.

“The platforms are taking the stance that unless a preference to the contrary is expressed then content is fair game for AI training. We should not be normalizing this view and need to continue to work to maintain the concept that the usage of content should be determined by its owners and the result of their direct permission,” Springer said.

This story has been updated to clarify that Google-Extended is a control publishers can use to restrict Google from using their content for AI models and tools, not a web crawler.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

#7241. Netflix embraces 35mm film in international campaign

“Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” consists of three new spots shot in different aspect ratios and a social series.

As U.S. consumers continue to cut back on subscriptions, including for streaming services, Netflix’s latest campaign emphasizes the quality of its vast content library in order to help it stand out in a crowded field. “Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” comes as company executives continue to put an emphasis on value and engagement following price hikes and lower projected ad revenue.

Created by Netflix’s in-house shop the Brand Creative Studio and agency Mother, the campaign features three spots shot on 35mm film using different aspect ratios. In “Axolotl,” which is shot for widescreen, an axolotl leverages its quadrupedalism to escape its tank and turn the TV back on after its owners leave in the middle of a “Bridgerton” marathon. “Dawn” follows a vampire who braves the rapidly rising sun to find a working TV in order to watch “Love Is Blind.” In “Hypersleep,” an astronaut who is the only one awake while his fellow shipmates are in cryosleep, wakes someone up in order to show them the “Soda Pop” scene from “KPop Demon Hunters.”

The spots were directed by Vania Heymann and Gal Muggia of the production company Iconoclast. The nature of the ads allows for the featured program to be swapped out depending on the market. In Brazil, the show featured in “Dawn” is crime drama “Os Donos Do Jogo,” or “Rulers of Fortune” in English.

Over the course of four episodes, the social series “Typecast” will feature four creatives and showcase their somewhat unexpected Netflix fixations. The series kicks off with fashion designer Willy Chavarria and his show of choice “Love on the Spectrum.” Other guests include musician Teezo Touchdown, makeup artist Isamaya Ffrench and Randy Savvy of The Compton Cowboys. The videos were filmed in 16mm to fit the feel of the overall campaign.

The campaign’s use of analog film is part of a recent trend that has seen advertisers looking to run counter to the rise of artificial intelligence. Stop-motion animation has been of particular interest, with brands such as Levi’s and Prada embracing the artform. Netflix’s spots, which feature custom-built sets, follow a similar vein of appealing to the desire for human-made advertising.

Netflix grew revenue 12% year over year to $12.9 billion in Q3, per an earnings release. The company lowered its expected ad revenue for the year from $3 billion to $2.7 billion, a drop it attributed to a slower-than-expected rollout of some advertising capabilities.

#7239. How performance metrics are rewriting creator rate cards

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Any creator asking for media dollars better have the reporting to back up the request. Advertisers aren’t cutting checks just on follower count and virality anymore.

To ink a creator deal, advertisers are asking for cost per lead, cost per conversion, earned media value and other performance metrics. In some cases, some brands are asking for Instagram link clicks and engaged YouTube views up front to determine if a creator is worth the ad spend.

“They want to see if there’s actual traffic being driven. We’re seeing a stronger emphasis on accountability for sales for each creator,” said Kimberly Duman, a managing partner at Glossary Artist creator management firm.

Creator campaigns are more often being judged against traditional performance marketing math: cost per conversion, cost per lead and return on ad spend.

Consider it yet another signal of the creator economy mirroring programmatic advertising.

As the tech has gotten better, creator platforms and agencies alike have spent the last year rolling out AI-powered dashboards with years of historical performance data. These souped up control panels allow brands to see a creator’s engagement rate, follower count, conversion rate and other performance stats before a deal is ever made.

Now, follower count becomes a vanity metric as brands look for metrics that prove purchasing behavior.

“Cost per lead or cost per conversion. Simple as that,” said Joseph Perello, founder and CEO of Props, a performance-based creator marketing platform, later adding, “Every client has a cost per conversion benchmark.”

Naturally, that benchmark looks different for every client. But as the tech improves, advertisers are retooling their scorecard to determine how good of a salesperson a creator is, he said.

Or as Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP, sees it: “The way that we work with a lot of our creators is to look at the performance of their content before we contract them.”

Before deal talks start, the global creator and influencer agency HYDP evaluates a creator’s content within a 30-day window, including view counts, but excludes any viral content to set reasonable expectations, Stamatiou said.

View counts has its own set of challenges. YouTube overhauled its view count in August. Rather than a count metric starting at several seconds in, a video view is registered the moment a video starts to play.

Now, advertiseres want “engaged views” — at least Blue Hour influencer agency is asking for those stats up front, Matt Higgins, svp and head of strategy at Blue Hour told Digiday.

“The bigger point is what we should focus on when finding and negotiating with creators,” Higgins said of the YouTube changes. “We negotiate around audience fit, shares, and expected performance, with views as one input.”

The brand tracks clicks and sales, but places greater emphasis on creator-brand alignment. “Great content will always win,” Glossary’s Duman said.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

Apple is placing strict limits on its staffers’ AI token consumption. Should the marketing sector take a similar path?

Q4 credits may bring advertisers to Reddit, but concerns about measurement and its ad capabilities make it harder to secure year-round spend.

#7236. As publishers await click data, Google’s AI visibility push complicates the opt-out decision

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Google’s latest publisher carrot? AI visibility.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

The opt-out is a Search Console control that lets publishers exclude their content from Google’s AI features. The control follows the CMA’s first conduct requirement on Google, imposed over the summer. While the ruling was U.K.-only, Google opened it to sites worldwide on August 31. Page-level grounding controls are due by March 3, 2027.

But the controls are bundled: publishers can’t opt out of individual features. That leaves them weighing the potential visibility against the loss of control and waiting for click data to help judge the trade-off.

That click data, which Google is required to provide to U.K. publishers by Dec. 3, will give publishers a key missing piece of the puzzle. And given Google made the first requirements global, publishers hope it will provide click data globally too. Google hasn’t confirmed that.

Google has shown regulators and publishers that it is cooperating by sharing impression data: how often publisher content appears in AI features. But it has held back the click data, which would show whether those appearances actually send people to publishers’ sites. “That is the metric that will determine whether they [publishers] turn it off or not,” said Chris Dicker, CEO of Candr Media, which owns Trusted Reviews. “They’re not giving the metrics [currently] that actually mean anything, when they could have — because they’ve got those — they could have done it at the same time as impressions, but they haven’t.”

He added that “everyone is holding off” playing around with the new controls until they see the click data because they don’t want to turn off a large proportion of their activities.

Dicker expects Google to share the click data in the coming weeks, ahead of Black Friday, when publishers are unlikely to risk experimenting with opt-outs during a crucial sales period.

Meanwhile, Google has updated AI Mode and AI Overviews to show links more often and more prominently, added timely article carousels, and included links to related stories and more context around surfaced articles.

Google says these changes will help publishers better track, control and increase their content visibility. It’s also testing links from video creators’ and publishers’ names to their Search profiles, and says that its Preferred Sources — the feature that lets users pick and bookmark the specific websites and publishers they most trust — has grown sevenfold this year.

That visibility comes with a catch, though: the more valuable Google’s AI features become, the harder it may be to justify opting out.

For some publishers, that trade-off is already a live question. “We’re discussing the CMA controls and how we exercise those,” said Carly Steven, director of ecommerce and commercial editorial at the Daily Mail, at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event in October.

“We need to be so, so careful because just as we’re about to get the ability to remove articles on a URL basis, Google announces this new feature, and we have to ask ourselves: do we really not want to be there? It does still matter that we’re on the surfaces where users are. So it’s a very complicated picture.”

David Buttle, founder of publisher AI standards coalition SPUR, believes Google’s preferred approach is to provide enough referral traffic from its AI-related surfaces for publishers to agree to let Google use their content, rather than paying them each time it does so. That means it needs to continue providing some kind of value exchange. “The way they can achieve that within the context of the conduct requirement is to ensure they can deliver enough value in the form of traffic from those surfaces back to publishers, so that they can say yes voluntarily,” said Buttle.

Trying to maintain its exchange with publishers is tricky when its newest rival for information retrieval – OpenAI – isn’t constrained by the same legacy relationship. “ChatGPT doesn’t pay for content generally, and it doesn’t send any traffic out,” he added. “So Google’s approach is to try and intermediate as many clicks from Discover, which is a really important traffic source for publishers, and they’re serving summaries really aggressively in Discover, and bundling the control for that with the control for summaries everywhere else.”

For all the noise around opting out, publishers are unlikely to do so unless they can opt out in a more granular way, stressed Buttle. “What it [Google] can do, though, is play around with the interface and the product for Discover to juice the AI-intermediated referrals that come out of that to get as much traffic out of that, so if you’re a publisher, it makes that decision to opt out far harder,” he added.

For some publishers, the question is still how to assess the controls before deciding whether to use them. At The Economist Group, that process is only beginning.

“On the CMA point, we’re looking at it,” said Josh Muncke, vp of generative AI at The Economist Group on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies event earlier this month. “I don’t believe we’ve tested it yet, but we’re certainly looking at it.”

Whatever that decision is, it will ultimately hinge on two things: one is how much traffic Google sends The Economist. The other is how often its name appears in the AI answers at the top of the results, even when nobody clicks through. He added: There are second-order effects to turning these things off that aren’t just about the traffic. They’re about broader visibility in the world.”

Some of those effects are already on the table. Some publishers expect excluded sites to drop out of the Top Stories carousel Google now places inside AI Overviews and Google hasn’t said how its control treats that unit.

Reuters Digital Phil Andraos told Digiday in June that the lack of click data meant any decision could be slow and probably involve testing.

Muncke’s instinct runs the same way. He said he wouldn’t frame it as a single in-or-out call at a fixed point. “It will be a case more of thinking about where and how we can test, measure, and see the impacts, and make kind of like more structured, data-driven calls as we go,” he said.

The Professional Publishers Association (PPA) is appealing to the CMA to press Google to unbundle the controls so they apply to individual features, rather than solely site- and page-level, so that publishers can have a deeper level of control.

For example, a specific query answered by an AI Overview may replace a click a publisher would previously have received, while the same content could still be valuable in a broader, conversational AI search.

“You’re either opting out of search AI Mode, AI Overviews – basically all these different features, or none of them,” said PPA’s head of policy and Public Affairs Eilidh Wilson. “And this is something that we continue to hear is what most concerns publishers. We’ve been really wanting to land this with impact with the CMA, so we’ve agreed to do a further follow-up briefing with them on it to look at the different parts,” she added.

Major AI companies are backing a new AI training opt-out standard proposal for publishers, but it exposes deeper tensions over AI scraping.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

#7233. Ogilvy and CAA launch partnership to clone celebrity talent for AI ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Body doubles are a popular subject in horror cinema. Usually, the doppelganger is the villain of the piece. But now, one of the leading gatekeepers in Hollywood is set to begin working with creative agency Ogilvy to run brand campaigns featuring entirely AI-generated celebrity appearances.

The WPP agency has inked a partnership with Creative Artists Agency (CAA) to generate AI ads featuring the actors, athletes and high-profile creators on CAA’s roster.

“We are witnessing the ‘NIL moment’ between advertising and entertainment,” said Carol Reed, global chief innovation officer at Ogilvy, referencing the Name, Image and Likeness industry that has grown around college sports influencers in the U.S. in recent years. “We’ve created a system where the biggest stars can license verified digital replicas safely… unlocking unprecedented agility for brands and assured control for talent.”

Brooklyn Nine-Nine star Terry Crews is among the actors who have opted in. Reed declined to name clients using the system, but said several campaigns were already being planned. “We are in the process of negotiating a few pilots right now that should be going live towards the end of the year,” she said.

The solution, called Echo, gives Ogilvy access to material kept in “The Vault” an in-house digital scanning facility built for CAA by tech company Veritone four years ago. Ogilvy and CAA’s partnership was a year in the making, per Reed. The studio uses 204 cameras to capture every facet of an actor, athlete or creator — from their voice to body language, facial expressions and the way they walk.

That material, which is refreshed by CAA clients every 3-5 years, is already used for de-aging or post-production fixes in films. Now, the agency’s staffers can pull it into WPP Open, the system behind the holding company’s various endeavors in creative production, agentic media buying and self-service creative tools, for internal storyboarding, mock-ups or final creative assets.

While the negotiations between actors or creators, their representatives and Ogilvy’s clients will still play out the same as always, the partnership is expected to yield more assets than classic shoots, at a faster pace, and reducing cost-per-asset by 65-75%.

The combination of digital capture and AI content generation could “fundamentally change celebrity endorsement economics,” according to Gartner analyst Nicole Greene.

David Raichman, executive creative director and global AI creative lead at Ogilvy, called the system “the next frontier of content… more of it, faster, and – for the first time – more trusted, not less.”

If that sounds outlandish, consider the campaigns launched by Coca-Cola and Snickers earlier this year featuring soccer coach and CAA client José Mourinho — or rather, his digital clone. Ogilvy’s partnership with the agency, which Reed said could stretch to include other talent companies in the future, gives it the chance to scale up such projects.

Echo isn’t Ogilvy’s first effort in this realm. Five years ago, Ogilvy used digital scans of Bollywood star Shah Rukh Khan to advertise small businesses across India during the Hindu festival of Diwali. That project served as a kind of “prototype” for Echo, according to Reed.

She said the agency expects the partnership to be particularly useful for ads featuring sports personalities. “They’re locked into training and seasons, so there’s long periods where they’re not available,” she said. “Now they can kind of engage and work throughout a season where normally they wouldn’t be available.”

AI tools have become increasingly common in agency and in-house creative production efforts, though they’re typically deployed with performance media assets in mind — ads for digital display, paid social or mobile app environments — rather than campaign materials featuring a famous face. Though Reed said the agency expects to use the system initially for social assets, Ogilvy’s partnership gives it an edge for clients looking to take advantage of AI gains across the entire spectrum of advertising production.

“Agencies need a first-mover advantage right now,” said Greene.

For CAA, it’s a chance to “grow the pie,” according to Alex Shannon, the talent agency’s head of strategic development. Stars can take on commercial work unfettered by the schedule of their day job on the pitch or in the movie studio — allowing them to work with more brands overall, but also capitalize on moments when their profile might be highest, such as the climax to a sporting season or during the launch of a film or TV show.

“Now, brands have the ability to move at the speed of culture, and athletes are able to do even more to engage fans at times when that relevancy is so critically important,” she said.

The partnership between Ogilvy and CAA includes a framework designed to govern how and when the agency’s clients can use the likeness of talent. CAA clients own their digital likenesses, and are able to pick and choose which businesses and markets they’re happy to appear in ads for or formally endorse.

“Talent always gets to see everything and approve everything at the end,” said Reed. AI advertising materials will also include a watermark that clearly marks a video or image as both AI-generated and approved by the star in question. Reed said the measure is a means of reassuring consumers that “this isn’t a deep fake”, as well as staying ahead of future AI regulation. (The European Union, for example, has mandated gen AI providers put watermarks on content produced using their tools since August.)

CAA has been aggressive in defending the value of its clients’ likenesses, signing an agreement with YouTube to remove celeb deep fakes from the video platform in 2024. But while watermarks might keep Ogilvy clear of future AI rules, they might not aid consumer comprehension.

“The whole point of talent is to scale authentic relationships and credibility,” said Greene. Yet consumers, when asked, are typically cool on the idea of AI-generated glitterati hawking brands. Nearly half (49%) of Britons have a negative view of AI-generated actors appearing in ads, according to YouGov, while 92% said AI generated content makes it harder to trust information online in general.

Reed argued the system is principally an extension of Ogilvy’s “palette”, rather than a means of flooding the web with celebrity slop. “The intention is that still there is a human craft and a human behind it,” she said, adding that the agency still expects to recommend a traditional shoot when a situation demands it. “Authenticity is one of those things that is going to be even more important,” she said.

Reed said that the partnership could mean brands find it easier to work with several lower-profile names (and skip the usual scheduling difficulty) rather than shelling out for a single movie star. “I’m hoping that you’ll see more [brands] opening up to B-tier athletes,” she said.

That use case may prove particularly important to Ogilvy. CMOs place an increasingly high importance on their agencies’ ability to manage their large rosters of creators and influencers, and to do so in a cost-effective manner. Eliminating the need for a TikTok personality or podcaster to actually be on location could add a sharp edge to the agency’s current pitch.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

Apple is placing strict limits on its staffers’ AI token consumption. Should the marketing sector take a similar path?

Q4 credits may bring advertisers to Reddit, but concerns about measurement and its ad capabilities make it harder to secure year-round spend.

#7229. Why publishers must act now to protect content from AI scrapers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Each technology wave brings the same promise: reach, relevance and a seat at the table. When the web arrived, publishers posted content to gain exposure, then spent a decade trying to claw back the revenue they gave away. When social media arrived, publishers threw open their archives to platforms that captured attention, data and advertising dollars. But each wave has left publishers with lower revenues, smaller newsrooms and a greater dependence on big tech platforms.

AI is the latest and potentially most disruptive wave. AI companies have spent four years crawling the web at industrial scale, ingesting decades of journalism to train models that answer questions directly instead of sending readers back to the source. Content that publishers built and funded is being taken without permission, compensation or attribution. AI companies have little incentive to pay while they can access publisher content for free.

Media leaders understand the risk. Yet research from digital analytics and customer engagement solutions provider Piano shows that fewer than 20% of the company’s 500 publisher customers use aggressive AI-bot blocking, while 30% use none at all. Without blocking, there’s no leverage to negotiate.

Some publishers worry that blocking AI means losing the chance to be discovered through AI. But less than 0.1% of publisher traffic comes from AI tools, according to data from Piano. Large language models keep users inside their own environments, so most readers never click through to the source. Anthropic sends back one visit for every 8,692 pages it scrapes; Perplexity, one for every 369. Until those numbers change, appearing in AI results doesn’t bring meaningful value.

Google’s pivot to AI Overviews has added to the pressure. Since AI Overviews launched in May 2024, clicks to publisher sites are down 49%. At Google I/O 2026 in May, Google unveiled a more AI-centric search experience that puts its chatbot-style AI Mode at the center of the search box, potentially pushing the traditional 10 blue links further down the page.

If organic search — currently 25% of traffic to Piano customers, according to internal benchmark data — continues to erode, the economic case for producing original content could collapse. Likewise, as long as AI companies can scrape content for free, they have no incentive to pay.

The path to compensation in the AI era runs through scarcity — and scarcity requires taking action like blocking bots and locking down content to create leverage. Then, publishers can negotiate.Here are four steps publishers can take now to create a path to compensation in the AI era.

Publishers can limit AI companies’ access to their content by declaring exclusions in robots.txt, blocking at the content delivery network (CDN) level, and locking paywalls to recognize only human visitors.

Some AI companies respect publisher rules, but many crawlers don’t. Piano’s analysis of Cloudflare data shows 70% of bot traffic is unverified. Perplexity has been documented circumventing blocking by impersonating Chrome browsers. Tavily openly admits its crawler skips a distinctive user agent specifically to dodge rules that let only Google crawl a site.

Basic bot-blocking may not be enough. Content protection ultimately means locking content for everyone, human or not, because the industry can’t reliably distinguish AI agents from human readers. One of the most secure approaches is requiring all readers to register, using server-side or CDN edge protection to block content from any unknown user.

An agentic commerce future in which AI systems pay publishers per crawl, per answer or per piece of content is real, but it’s not here yet. Platforms like TollBit and Pro Rata and standards like x402 and IAB’s Content Monetization Protocols are developing the frameworks. But no one is making meaningful money from bot marketplaces yet.

A defensive strategy won’t drive growth on its own. The same AI companies threatening publishers also enable valuable tools, including personalization at scale and AI-powered subscription monetization. The next generation of predictive systems will auto-optimize in real time, cutting manual work.

At the same time, an industry that spent 20 years unable to coordinate is starting to unify around how its content is used. The Standards for Publisher Usage Rights (SPUR) initiative is the clearest signal: 30 new members joined at WAN-IFRA’s World News Media Congress in June. The Real Simple Licensing Collective and the News Media Alliance are building similar infrastructure.

Publishers are also taking a harder line on how AI companies access their content. The Atlantic and People Inc. have put aggressive bot-blocking into practice. At the WAN-IFRA June conference, The New York Times’ publisher called on the industry to stop giving content away and demand fair terms.

Publishers should use AI where it creates value while retaining control over the content that powers these systems. The right move is the same one the best publishers made when subscriptions became necessary: be decisive, be clear about the value offered and build a business that doesn’t depend on someone else’s goodwill.The Publisher’s Content Protection Playbook from Piano covers practical guidance on blocking scrapers, how to know what content is worth before licensing it to AI companies, how to build leverage in AI licensing conversations and, how Piano protects content, creates personalized experiences and builds stronger reader relationships.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

Apple is placing strict limits on its staffers’ AI token consumption. Should the marketing sector take a similar path?

Q4 credits may bring advertisers to Reddit, but concerns about measurement and its ad capabilities make it harder to secure year-round spend.

Get access to tools and analysis to stay ahead of the trends transforming media and marketing

Follow @Digiday for the latest news, insider access to events and more.

#7226. ‘We are looking more now’: The Trade Desk is eyeing the M&A aisle

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The Trade Desk has spent a decade as a public company buying close to nothing. On Monday night, CEO Jeff Green suggested that streak is up for review.

Asked about M&A on the company’s third quarter earnings call, the ad tech boss said the demand-side platform is combining the market for deals more closely than at any point in its history. He said: “Our philosophy on M&A has not fundamentally changed. We are always looking, and I will just transparently say we are looking more now than we historically have, partly because I just think there is a bunch of opportunity in the market that there has not been in the past.”

For a company whose deal record fits on an index card, that’s quite the admission.

The Trade Desk has announced two acquisitions since its 2016 IPO. It bought Adbrain, a London-based identity resolution company, in October 2017. Sincera, a New York ad data startup The Trade Desk had already backed through its venture arm, followed in January 2025. AdExchanger reported a third deal in between, though the target was never named. Terms of the Sincera deal weren’t disclosed either. The Trade Desk’s cash flow statement shows $4.35 million spent on a business acquisition in the first nine months of 2025, the window in which Sincera closed.

Neither announced deal set off a spending spree and Green went out of his way to keep it that way. Weeks after the Sincera news broke, he used his slot at the IAB’s Annual Leadership Meeting to squash talk of a new acquisition strategy. Sincera’s team was small, he told the room, before steering the conversation back to the tech The Trade Desk builds itself. Nearly two years on, the M&A philosophy is officially unchanged. The amount of looking is not.

“We do not ever want to lose sight of the opportunity in front of us and do not want to get distracted through M&A, but at the same time recognize that that can help accelerate us,” said Green. “We are always looking.”

What those targets are, Green declined to say. Most of his answer went on the bar that a deal would have to cleared, which he said is higher than it has ever been. In other words, anything the company bought would have to fit the turnaround already underway.

“It is also critical to take away that our standards are also higher than ever,” Green continued. “The reason why our standards are higher than ever is we have an opportunity to execute and accelerate our growth with all the changes that we have made in our business. I would never want to derail that by making our 17th priority our number one priority simply because we bought a company. By being super focused on the things that we look at and hold that acquisition standard to a very high level, we think we create the clearest opportunities ahead.

If The Trade Desk was ever going to break the habit of a lifetime, now would be the time. Revenue has gone backwards for the first time since the IPO, its guidance for the fourth quarter sits about 11% below last year’s and Amazon continues to eat into its share of the market. Green has spent the year rebuilding from the inside with updates to the buying platform, a tighter focus on measurement and a 15% workforce cut. But building alone hasn’t fixed the business fast enough and critics have spent months pointing to holes a purchase could plug, from its own identity data to retail transaction signals.

The money to fill those gaps is already in the bank. The company ended the third quarter with $1.23 billion in cash and cash equivalents, plus another $420 million in short-term investments with no debt on its balance sheet.

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.

#7222. Amid a difficult transitional year, The Trade Desk posts a rare quarterly revenue decline

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The latest batch of ad tech earnings disclosures was made public after the bell yesterday, with The Trade Desk posting a rare annual revenue decrease, only the second in its decade as a publicly listed company, and a sign of the scale of its turnaround challenges.

The industry’s largest independent demand-side platform, whose immediate rivals are Amazon and Google, recently trumpeted the return of The Trade Desk cofounder and CTO Dave Pickles to help mount its comeback after a challenging 18-to-24 months.

The DSP posted Q3 revenue of $736 million, representing a 0.5% annual dip – the last such dip occurred at the depths of business uncertainty during the Covid-19 pandemic – a sign of the scale of the turnaround challenge it faces.

Meanwhile, its ad tech cohorts posted revenue increases during the reporting period on the same day (see chart below), another sign of the sector’s mixed fortunes.

Jeff Green, cofounder and CEO, The Trade Desk, noted the company’s reorganization efforts of the last year, which included a 15% headcount cut during Q3, plus its third-party audience and agentic offerings, plus its plans for more transparent measurement for advertisers. “I’m extremely excited to have him [Pickles] more deeply engaged with the team as we move into this next chapter,” Green told analysts, “we spent the last year changing our company because the industry has changed and is continuing to change.”

Execs there pointed out that revenue performance during the period was “meaningfully ahead of the outlook we provided,” noting how video, including CTV, now accounts for more than half of its revenue mix, with the DSP also forecasting that its Q4 revenue would be “at least $755 million.”

In a prepared statement, Jeff Green said, “We are moving with greater urgency across the company,” but acknowledged, “we are still operating below the level of growth we expect from ourselves.”

Facing questions from analysts, Green later outlined the company’s planned tactical changes, as well as when it expects to return to more sustainable growth, noting how the company needs to “make the power of The Trade Desk easier to use, easier to adopt and easier to prove incremental outcomes.”

He added, “Open measurement is expected to better prove incremental outcomes… while open measurement will be implemented this quarter, we expect the product to improve our growth next year and provide a tailwind for years to come.”

Under further questioning from analysts, Green noted how The Trade Desk – a company that has been notably reticent over mergers and acquisitions – was more open than ever to M&A given the current technological landscape.

However, that shouldn’t be mistaken for a change in the company’s attitude to writing checks for more ad tech, even if it does lead to more lines of business. “Our philosophy on M&A has not fundamentally changed,” he told analysts.

“We’re looking [at M&A prospects] more now than we historically have, but that said, it’s also critical to take away that our standards are also higher than ever,” Green said, adding that his leadership would never want to “derail” its current trajectory “simply because we bought a company.”

Green’s comments came the same afternoon as Zeta Global posted revenue of $493 million, up 46% year on year, making it one of the fastest-growing companies in terms of revenue throughout the period, with the company further raising its Q4 revenue guidance to the range of $521 million to $525 million, a figure that would represent up to 33% annual growth.

Meanwhile, rival DSP Viant posted $110.4 million in Q3 revenue, a 29% annual increase, as well as the purchase of generative AI company Waymark, with management noting they expected Q4 revenue in the region of $130.5 million to $133.5 million.

Elsewhere, supply-side platform PubMatic posted revenue of $79.6 million, up 17% year on year, with company leadership keen to underline an ongoing industry trend that many feel is helping to bring more equity to the historic power imbalance between the buy- and sell-side of the industry.

“AI is accelerating the shift from sell-side targeting to sell-side decisioning, enabling buyers to act on richer intelligence closer to the inventory and first party [sic] insights,” noted Rajeev Goel, PubMatic CEO, in a prepared statement, which also forecast Q4 revenue in the range of $91 to $93 million, a prospective increase of 15%.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

Apple is placing strict limits on its staffers’ AI token consumption. Should the marketing sector take a similar path?

Q4 credits may bring advertisers to Reddit, but concerns about measurement and its ad capabilities make it harder to secure year-round spend.

#7219. Ad Tech Briefing: Amazon’s auction trial puts agentic advertising’s trust problem in focus

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Amazon, along with its Big Tech cohorts, wants advertisers to trust its AI more, but fresh allegations from federal regulators underline concerns over such platforms.

Last week, the Federal Trade Commission published newly unredacted details from its lawsuit accusing Amazon of secretly inflating advertising prices, with similar charges against Google still fresh in the public mindset.

According to the FTC, Amazon imposed surcharges of up to 50% on ordinary shopping days and 250% during major retail events, generating an additional $87 million during the 2022 holiday season. Amazon disputes the allegations, and the legal proceedings are ongoing.

Amazon recently reported quarterly advertising revenue approaching $20 billion, with  Amazon Ads Agent – an AI-powered platform that’s likely to attract accusations of “black box” from detractors – a key part of its push to automate campaign planning, buying and optimization.

And with Amazon, the third pillar of adland’s triopoly, the key question for advertisers is whether they should hand over more control to a platform when they cannot independently verify how its existing auctions determine prices.

The FTC and 22 state attorneys general accuse Amazon of misleading advertisers about its Sponsored Products auctions.

Amazon presented these as generalized second-price auctions, where advertisers generally pay prices determined by competing bids rather than their maximum bid.

However, regulators allege Amazon introduced undisclosed reserve-price mechanisms that increased charges without corresponding competition, effectively pushing prices closer to first-price auctions.

According to the government body’s allegations, during the 2021 holiday season, Amazon increased average cost per click by 20%, with some advertisers seeing increases exceeding 200%. The complaint also alleges executives considered using reserve prices to address a $40 million revenue shortfall.

The allegations extend to how Amazon explained rising prices, i.e., whether Amazon misrepresented how its auctions worked and what advertisers were charged.

However, Amazon maintains that reserve prices reflect the value of its advertising placements, disputes the FTC’s interpretation, and argues that reserve pricing is not inherently unlawful.

For advertisers, the concern is straightforward: they can see what they paid, but cannot necessarily establish how that price was calculated.

The allegations come shortly after Amazon introduced new products designed to automate more advertising decisions.

At October’s unBoxed conference, Amazon unveiled Ads Agent and Full-Funnel Campaigns, allowing advertisers to set objectives, products, creative and budgets while its technology handles targeting, allocation and optimization.

Amazon says beta testing delivered up to 67% higher return on long-term ad spend and 29% lower costs per new-to-brand customer compared with manually configured campaigns. Those figures are company-reported.

The proposition is that advertisers can reduce manual campaign management and improve performance. However, greater automation also means relying more heavily on Amazon to explain how budgets are spent and why its systems make particular decisions.

While the FTC’s allegations concern Sponsored Products auctions, not Amazon’s new AI products, they still add weight to concerns raised about advertisers’ ability to inspect automated decisions, verify results, and understand fees.

Google’s ad tech antitrust case provides a precedent, although the conduct and markets differ.

A federal court found Google illegally monopolized publisher ad-serving and ad exchange markets – in a separate case, its search practices were also found to be monopolistic. The subsequent remedies stopped short of divestiture but imposed measures intended to improve competition and interoperability.

Both cases concern platforms whose control over advertising infrastructure limits customers’ ability to scrutinize how transactions work.

The difference is that Amazon is now asking advertisers to give its technology greater control over buying decisions, even as regulators challenge the transparency of its existing auction practices. These developments involve different businesses and issues, but they point to a broader accountability problem in digital advertising.

“We’re having to look much more closely at what happens after the click and whether those clicks are actually producing qualified leads and revenue.”

— Lindsay Marty, founder of Above the Bar Marketing, speaking to Digiday about advertisers reassessing their Google Search budgets.

ChatGPT’s ad offering may be on the rise, but the nature of its value is still hard to determine, according to numbers analyzed in recent studies.

⁠Amazon’s Ads nears $20 billion in Q3 as it bets on agentic AI to fuel further growth

Amazon’s new automated buying tools promise simplified execution while raising questions about transparency and advertiser control.

Alphabet’s latest earnings reveal how AI is complicating the economics of search advertising even as its cloud business expands.

⁠Taboola faces Yahoo mediation over its 30-year native ad deal

A dispute involving one of Taboola’s most important commercial relationships emerges alongside disappointing quarterly revenue.

AI-driven tech layoffs suck, but might lead to certification

Former Google ads leader, Jonathan Bellack, on his Platformocracy outlet, examines how AI coding threatens junior engineering career paths and argues that professional certification could become increasingly important.

⁠Agencies find AI overuse is leading to brief bloat

Agency executives warn that AI-generated briefs are creating more work rather than improving productivity.

Media Briefing: Publishers’ revenue growth defies traffic declines, but tougher comparisons loom

Major publishers continue to grow revenue despite deteriorating referral traffic and mounting AI disruption.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.

#7215. Can web users trust ad tech’s server-side shift?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

“Trust” is one of those words that gets thrown around a lot in ad tech circles. But it’s going to become even more important if ad tech completes its server-side shift.

In response to Apple’s identity resolution crackdown, the online ad industry has rallied around server-side ways to sidestep browser-based controls. IAB Tech Lab’s Trusted Server is the biggest example of this. Not only does the open-source software promise publishers faster page-load speeds, it enables publishers to better control their ad tech stacks and minimize their exposure to browser-based blockades.

Taking ad calls out of the browser, however, has the effect of taking them out of users’ sights. A browser can see – and block – the calls a web page asks it to make. Once the calls run on a publisher’s server, though, users have to trust the publisher to a greater degree.

And this is where the trust in Trusted Server really matters.

Lam also noted that Trusted Server was designed from the start to support integration with consent management platforms. That would give websites a way to share a user’s consent status across the programmatic supply chain. But of course, that requires the user blindly trusting the site to do so.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

“There is a fear of just broadly server-side because it’s not automatically observable to the consumer’s browser and you can’t automatically inspect it from that point. But what we’ve done for Trusted Server in particular is we’ve made sure all of this is considered. What’s included in Trusted Server is pretty robust logging and auditing. Anyone who is looking to deploy Trusted Server gets that out of the box.”

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.
